I guess… the main reason I came in is that I just can’t get out from under this feeling that I’ve really messed everything up. Like I’ve fallen behind at work, and I know people depend on me there, and then things at my apartment have gotten out of control too, and it all feels like it’s my fault.

It’s not just feeling stressed or embarrassed. It’s more like this heavy certainty that I’ve ruined things for everyone around me, and that whatever happens next—if something bad happens—I probably deserve it. I know that may sound dramatic, but that’s honestly where my head keeps going.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Not really, no. I mean… I still try sometimes, because I remember those things used to help. Like I’ll put on a record I used to love, or I’ll start cooking something that normally would’ve felt kind of grounding, or I’ll pull out some little electronics thing I was going to tinker with. But most days I wake up with this flat, heavy sadness, and those things just don’t seem to reach me anymore.

It’s strange because I can remember liking them. I know, objectively, that they were things I enjoyed. But when I do them now, it’s like there’s a wall there. I’m going through the motions, waiting for that little feeling of relief or interest to show up, and it just… doesn’t.

Not like I used to, no. I mean, I still care about them, I know I do, and sometimes I’ll answer a text or sit with people if they come by, but it doesn’t really feel… relieving, I guess.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
A little, maybe… but not in the way I used to. It’s hard to explain without sounding like I’m being dramatic, but most days I wake up already feeling this flat, heavy sadness, before I’ve even talked to anyone or done anything. So by the time I’m around people, it’s like I’m already behind this thick pane of glass.

I can sit there and know, like, “These are people I care about, this should feel nice, I should be glad they’re here,” but the feeling doesn’t really come through. It’s kind of the same as with the other things I used to rely on to reset myself—putting on a record, cooking something, tinkering with some little electronics project. I still recognize them as good things, but they don’t reach me the way they used to.

So… I wouldn’t say I enjoy company much anymore. I can tolerate it sometimes, and I don’t want to hurt anyone’s feelings, but it doesn’t lift that heaviness. It just stays there.

It’s been pretty steadily bad for a long time, honestly. I don’t know if I’d separate it into a neat “few weeks” where it was worse, because it kind of… settled in and stayed.

My routines fell apart, the little structure I had during the day disappeared, and since then it’s just felt like I haven’t really gotten back to myself.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Not really, no. I keep trying the same things because part of me remembers that they used to help. Like, I’ll put on a record and think maybe if I just sit with it long enough something will click, or I’ll start cooking something because that used to feel grounding, like there was a rhythm to it. Or I’ll pull out some little electronics thing to tinker with, because that used to make me lose track of time in a good way.

But most days I wake up with this flat, heavy sadness already there, and it’s like everything has to pass through that first. The music is still music, the food still smells like it should, the project still makes sense in my head, but it just doesn’t… reach me. I can recognize that these are things I used to enjoy, and sometimes I go through the motions because I don’t know what else to do, but the actual pleasure part is mostly missing.

It’s hard to explain without sounding like I’m being dramatic, but most days I wake up already feeling this flat, heavy sadness, before I’ve even talked to anyone or done anything. So by the time I’m around people, it’s like I’m already behind this thick pane of glass.\n\nI can sit there and know, like, “These are people I care about, this should feel nice, I should be glad they’re here,” but the feeling doesn’t really come through. It’s kind of the same as with the other things I used to rely on to reset myself—putting on a record, cooking something, tinkering with some little electronics project. I still recognize them as good things, but they don’t reach me the way they used to.\n\nSo… I wouldn’t say I enjoy company much anymore. I can tolerate it sometimes, and I don’t want to hurt anyone’s feelings, but it doesn’t lift that heaviness. It just stays there.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any periods in the last year where your symptoms were significantly worse for a few weeks?\n\nPatient: It’s been pretty steadily bad for a long time, honestly. I don’t know if I’d separate it into a neat “few weeks” where it was worse, because it kind of… settled in and stayed.\n\nI’d say this has been going on for about a year and two months now. It started after that pipe burst forced me out of my place and into the smaller apartment. That sounds like such a practical thing, like just a housing problem, but it threw everything off. My routines fell apart, the little structure I had during the day disappeared, and since then it’s just felt like I haven’t really gotten back to myself.\n\nSo, yes, there have been times where it felt heavier, but the bigger thing is that it’s been there for about fourteen months, pretty continuously.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still find pleasure in?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I keep trying the same things because part of me remembers that they used to help. Like, I’ll put on a record and think maybe if I just sit with it long enough something will click, or I’ll start cooking something because that used to feel grounding, like there was a rhythm to it. Or I’ll pull out some little electronics thing to tinker with, because that used to make me lose track of time in a good way.\n\nBut most days I wake up with this flat, heavy sadness already there, and it’s like everything has to pass through that first. The music is still music, the food still smells like it should, the project still makes sense in my head, but it just doesn’t… reach me. I can recognize that these are things I used to enjoy, and sometimes I go through the motions because I don’t know what else to do, but the actual pleasure part is mostly missing.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you had times in the last year where you felt somewhat better for a while, before the sadness came back?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Not really in any clear way, no. I mean, there are probably individual hours or maybe a day here and there where I can function a little better, or I’m distracted enough that I think, “Maybe I’m coming out of it,” but it doesn’t last long enough to feel like an actual better stretch.

The bigger thing is that it’s been going on for about a year and two months now, pretty much since the pipe burst and I had to move into that smaller apartment. I keep coming back to that because it’s when everything seemed to come apart. My routines were disrupted, the space felt wrong, and I never really got my footing back after that.

So I wouldn’t say there was a period where I got better and then the sadness came back. It’s more like it settled in around then and has just stayed, even if some days are a little less heavy than others.

I keep trying the same things because part of me remembers that they used to help. Like, I’ll put on a record and think maybe if I just sit with it long enough something will click, or I’ll start cooking something because that used to feel grounding, like there was a rhythm to it. Or I’ll pull out some little electronics thing to tinker with, because that used to make me lose track of time in a good way.\n\nBut most days I wake up with this flat, heavy sadness already there, and it’s like everything has to pass through that first. The music is still music, the food still smells like it should, the project still makes sense in my head, but it just doesn’t… reach me. I can recognize that these are things I used to enjoy, and sometimes I go through the motions because I don’t know what else to do, but the actual pleasure part is mostly missing.\n\nDoctor: Have you had times in the last year where you felt somewhat better for a while, before the sadness came back?\n\nPatient: Not really in any clear way, no. I mean, there are probably individual hours or maybe a day here and there where I can function a little better, or I’m distracted enough that I think, “Maybe I’m coming out of it,” but it doesn’t last long enough to feel like an actual better stretch.\n\nThe bigger thing is that it’s been going on for about a year and two months now, pretty much since the pipe burst and I had to move into that smaller apartment. I keep coming back to that because it’s when everything seemed to come apart. My routines were disrupted, the space felt wrong, and I never really got my footing back after that.\n\nSo I wouldn’t say there was a period where I got better and then the sadness came back. I think… yes and no. It’s hard to measure because it didn’t feel like one straight drop every month, like I could point to March versus July and say, “There, that was worse.” It’s more that this has been going on for about a year and two months now, ever since the pipe burst and I had to leave my place and move into that smaller apartment.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
No, I don’t think so. Not completely back to normal.

There have been little pockets where I’ve been less aware of it, maybe, or I’ve managed to get through a day without everything feeling quite as stuck. But if you mean really feeling like myself again, like before all of this started, then no. This has been going on for about a year and two months now, since the pipe burst and I had to move into that smaller apartment. I keep mentioning that because it feels like the point where my routines fell apart, and they just never really came back together.

So even on the better days, I still don’t feel like I’m back to the way I was. It’s more like I’m functioning around it, not actually normal again.

I mean, I still care about them, I know I do, and sometimes I’ll answer a text or sit with people if they come by, but it doesn’t really feel… relieving, I guess.\n\nMost days I wake up with this flat, heavy sadness already there, before anything has even happened, and it kind of colors everything. The things that used to reset me—putting on a record, cooking something, messing around with electronics—don’t really reach me anymore, and it’s similar with people. I can be in the room with friends or family and know, intellectually, that this should feel good or comforting, but inside it’s just kind of dull and heavy. I’m there, but I don’t feel connected to it the way I used to.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're able to enjoy the company of others at all anymore?\n\nPatient: A little, maybe… but not in the way I used to. It’s hard to explain without sounding like I’m being dramatic, but most days I wake up already feeling this flat, heavy sadness, before I’ve even talked to anyone or done anything. So by the time I’m around people, it’s like I’m already behind this thick pane of glass.\n\nI can sit there and know, like, “These are people I care about, this should feel nice, I should be glad they’re here,” but the feeling doesn’t really come through. It’s kind of the same as with the other things I used to rely on to reset myself—putting on a record, cooking something, tinkering with some little electronics project. I still recognize them as good things, but they don’t reach me the way they used to.\n\nSo… I wouldn’t say I enjoy company much anymore. I can tolerate it sometimes, and I don’t want to hurt anyone’s feelings, but it doesn’t lift that heaviness. It just stays there.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any periods in the last year where your symptoms were significantly worse for a few weeks?\n\nPatient: It’s been pretty steadily bad for a long time, honestly. I don’t know if I’d separate it into a neat “few weeks” where it was worse, because it kind of… settled in and stayed.\n\nI’d say this has been going on for about a year and two months now. It started after that pipe burst forced me out of my place and into the smaller apartment. That sounds like such a practical thing, like just a housing problem, but it threw everything off. My routines fell apart, the little structure I had during the day disappeared, and since then it’s just felt like I haven’t really gotten back to myself.\n\nSo, yes, there have been times where it felt heavier, but the bigger thing is that it’s been there for about fourteen months, pretty continuously.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still find pleasure in?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I keep trying the same things because part of me remembers that they used to help. Like, I’ll put on a record and think maybe if I just sit with it long enough something will click, or I’ll start cooking something because that used to feel grounding, like there was a rhythm to it. Or I’ll pull out some little electronics thing to tinker with, because that used to make me lose track of time in a good way.\n\nBut most days I wake up with this flat, heavy sadness already there, and it’s like everything has to pass through that first. The music is still music, the food still smells like it should, the project still makes sense in my head, but it just doesn’t… reach me. I can recognize that these are things I used to enjoy, and sometimes I go through the motions because I don’t know what else to do, but the actual pleasure part is mostly missing.\n\nDoctor: Have you had times in the last year where you felt somewhat better for a while, before the sadness came back?\n\nPatient: Not really in any clear way, no. I mean, there are probably individual hours or maybe a day here and there where I can function a little better, or I’m distracted enough that I think, “Maybe I’m coming out of it,” but it doesn’t last long enough to feel like an actual better stretch.\n\nThe bigger thing is that it’s been going on for about a year and two months now, pretty much since the pipe burst and I had to move into that smaller apartment. I keep coming back to that because it’s when everything seemed to come apart. My routines were disrupted, the space felt wrong, and I never really got my footing back after that.\n\nSo I wouldn’t say there was a period where I got better and then the sadness came back. It’s more like it settled in around then and has just stayed, even if some days are a little less heavy than others.\n\nDoctor: Have your symptoms gotten worse over the past fourteen months?\n\nPatient: I think… yes and no. It’s hard to measure because it didn’t feel like one straight drop every month, like I could point to March versus July and say, “There, that was worse.” It’s more that this has been going on for about a year and two months now, ever since the pipe burst and I had to leave my place and move into that smaller apartment.\n\nAt first I kept thinking it was just the disruption, you know, like once I unpacked or got used to the new space, I’d settle back into my routines. But the routines kind of fell apart and never really came back the same way. So over those fourteen months, it feels like it became more fixed, more settled in. Some days are heavier than others, but the main thing is that it started around then and has just kept going.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any periods in the last year where you felt completely back to normal?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. Not completely back to normal.\n\nThere have been little pockets where I’ve been less aware of it, maybe, or I’ve managed to get through a day without everything feeling quite as stuck. But if you mean really feeling like myself again, like before all of this started, then no. This has been going on for about a year and two months now, since the pipe burst and I had to move into that smaller apartment. I keep mentioning that because it feels like the point where my routines fell apart, and they just never really came back together.\n\nSo even on the better days, I still don’t feel like I’m back to the way I was. It’s more like I’m functioning around it, not actually normal again.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any distinct periods in the last year where you felt depressed, followed by periods where you felt okay again?\n\nPatient: No, not really distinct periods like that. I’ve had days where I’m a little more functional, or maybe I get through work or errands and it feels less intense for a while, but I wouldn’t say there was a real stretch where I felt okay again and then later got depressed again.\n\nIt’s more like… this started about a year and two months ago, when the pipe burst and I had to move into that smaller apartment, and after that my routines just fell apart. Since then it’s sort of been one long period, with some days better or worse, but not like separate episodes with normal time in between.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Not really, no. I mean, there are days where I’m distracted enough that I’m not actively thinking, “I’m sad,” every minute, but if I really check in with myself, most days I wake up with this flat, heavy kind of sadness already there.

It’s not always dramatic or crying or anything like that. It’s more like… everything has a weight to it before the day even starts. Most of the day, I think. It can sort of fade into the background if I’m busy or distracted, but it doesn’t really come and go in a clean way.

Most mornings I wake up and it’s already there, this flat, heavy sadness. Not always sharp or overwhelming, but like a weight over everything. And even when I try to do the things that used to help—putting on a record, cooking something, tinkering with some electronics—it’s like they don’t reach the same part of me anymore. I might go through the motions, but the feeling is still sitting there underneath.

So I wouldn’t say it just comes and goes. It’s usually present, maybe quieter at times, but still there.

The things that used to reset me—putting on a record, cooking something, messing around with electronics—don’t really reach me anymore, and it’s similar with people. I can be in the room with friends or family and know, intellectually, that this should feel good or comforting, but inside it’s just kind of dull and heavy. I’m there, but I don’t feel connected to it the way I used to.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're able to enjoy the company of others at all anymore?\n\nPatient: A little, maybe… but not in the way I used to. It’s hard to explain without sounding like I’m being dramatic, but most days I wake up already feeling this flat, heavy sadness, before I’ve even talked to anyone or done anything. So by the time I’m around people, it’s like I’m already behind this thick pane of glass.\n\nI can sit there and know, like, “These are people I care about, this should feel nice, I should be glad they’re here,” but the feeling doesn’t really come through. It’s kind of the same as with the other things I used to rely on to reset myself—putting on a record, cooking something, tinkering with some little electronics project. I still recognize them as good things, but they don’t reach me the way they used to.\n\nSo… I wouldn’t say I enjoy company much anymore. I can tolerate it sometimes, and I don’t want to hurt anyone’s feelings, but it doesn’t lift that heaviness. It just stays there.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any periods in the last year where your symptoms were significantly worse for a few weeks?\n\nPatient: It’s been pretty steadily bad for a long time, honestly. I don’t know if I’d separate it into a neat “few weeks” where it was worse, because it kind of… settled in and stayed.\n\nI’d say this has been going on for about a year and two months now. It started after that pipe burst forced me out of my place and into the smaller apartment. That sounds like such a practical thing, like just a housing problem, but it threw everything off. My routines fell apart, the little structure I had during the day disappeared, and since then it’s just felt like I haven’t really gotten back to myself.\n\nSo, yes, there have been times where it felt heavier, but the bigger thing is that it’s been there for about fourteen months, pretty continuously.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still find pleasure in?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I keep trying the same things because part of me remembers that they used to help. Like, I’ll put on a record and think maybe if I just sit with it long enough something will click, or I’ll start cooking something because that used to feel grounding, like there was a rhythm to it. Or I’ll pull out some little electronics thing to tinker with, because that used to make me lose track of time in a good way.\n\nBut most days I wake up with this flat, heavy sadness already there, and it’s like everything has to pass through that first. The music is still music, the food still smells like it should, the project still makes sense in my head, but it just doesn’t… reach me. I can recognize that these are things I used to enjoy, and sometimes I go through the motions because I don’t know what else to do, but the actual pleasure part is mostly missing.\n\nDoctor: Have you had times in the last year where you felt somewhat better for a while, before the sadness came back?\n\nPatient: Not really in any clear way, no. I mean, there are probably individual hours or maybe a day here and there where I can function a little better, or I’m distracted enough that I think, “Maybe I’m coming out of it,” but it doesn’t last long enough to feel like an actual better stretch.\n\nThe bigger thing is that it’s been going on for about a year and two months now, pretty much since the pipe burst and I had to move into that smaller apartment. I keep coming back to that because it’s when everything seemed to come apart. My routines were disrupted, the space felt wrong, and I never really got my footing back after that.\n\nSo I wouldn’t say there was a period where I got better and then the sadness came back. It’s more like it settled in around then and has just stayed, even if some days are a little less heavy than others.\n\nDoctor: Have your symptoms gotten worse over the past fourteen months?\n\nPatient: I think… yes and no. It’s hard to measure because it didn’t feel like one straight drop every month, like I could point to March versus July and say, “There, that was worse.” It’s more that this has been going on for about a year and two months now, ever since the pipe burst and I had to leave my place and move into that smaller apartment.\n\nAt first I kept thinking it was just the disruption, you know, like once I unpacked or got used to the new space, I’d settle back into my routines. But the routines kind of fell apart and never really came back the same way. So over those fourteen months, it feels like it became more fixed, more settled in. Some days are heavier than others, but the main thing is that it started around then and has just kept going.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any periods in the last year where you felt completely back to normal?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. Not completely back to normal.\n\nThere have been little pockets where I’ve been less aware of it, maybe, or I’ve managed to get through a day without everything feeling quite as stuck. But if you mean really feeling like myself again, like before all of this started, then no. This has been going on for about a year and two months now, since the pipe burst and I had to move into that smaller apartment. I keep mentioning that because it feels like the point where my routines fell apart, and they just never really came back together.\n\nSo even on the better days, I still don’t feel like I’m back to the way I was. It’s more like I’m functioning around it, not actually normal again.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any distinct periods in the last year where you felt depressed, followed by periods where you felt okay again?\n\nPatient: No, not really distinct periods like that. I’ve had days where I’m a little more functional, or maybe I get through work or errands and it feels less intense for a while, but I wouldn’t say there was a real stretch where I felt okay again and then later got depressed again.\n\nIt’s more like… this started about a year and two months ago, when the pipe burst and I had to move into that smaller apartment, and after that my routines just fell apart. Since then it’s sort of been one long period, with some days better or worse, but not like separate episodes with normal time in between.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any days in the last year where you didn't feel sad or heavy at all?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, there are days where I’m distracted enough that I’m not actively thinking, “I’m sad,” every minute, but if I really check in with myself, most days I wake up with this flat, heavy kind of sadness already there.\n\nIt’s not always dramatic or crying or anything like that. It’s more like… everything has a weight to it before the day even starts. The things that used to sort of reset me don’t seem to work the same way anymore. I used to put on a record and actually feel it change the room a little, or I’d cook something, or mess around with some electronics project, and it would pull me back into myself. Lately those things just don’t reach me. I can do them, sometimes, but it’s like they don’t get through.\n\nSo I don’t think I’ve had a day where there was none of that heaviness at all. Maybe lighter days, but not completely gone.\n\nDoctor: Are your feelings of sadness usually present for most of the day, or do they come and go?\n\nPatient: Most of the day, I think. It can sort of fade into the background if I’m busy or distracted, but it doesn’t really come and go in a clean way.\n\nMost mornings I wake up and it’s already there, this flat, heavy sadness. Not always sharp or overwhelming, but like a weight over everything. And even when I try to do the things that used to help—putting on a record, cooking something, tinkering with some electronics—it’s like they don’t reach the same part of me anymore. I might go through the motions, but the feeling is still sitting there underneath.\n\nSo I wouldn’t say it just comes and goes. It’s usually present, maybe quieter at times, but still there.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

Lately those things just don’t reach me. I can do them, sometimes, but it’s like they don’t get through.\n\nSo I don’t think I’ve had a day where there was none of that heaviness at all. Maybe lighter days, but not completely gone.\n\nDoctor: Are your feelings of sadness usually present for most of the day, or do they come and go?\n\nPatient: Most of the day, I think. It can sort of fade into the background if I’m busy or distracted, but it doesn’t really come and go in a clean way.\n\nMost mornings I wake up and it’s already there, this flat, heavy sadness. Not always sharp or overwhelming, but like a weight over everything. And even when I try to do the things that used to help—putting on a record, cooking something, tinkering with some electronics—it’s like they don’t reach the same part of me anymore. I might go through the motions, but the feeling is still sitting there underneath.\n\nSo I wouldn’t say it just comes and goes. It’s usually present, maybe quieter at times, but still there.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave your symptoms lasted for two years or more?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 39 / computer_support_specialist / bachelors\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Duration\nmanifestation: \"This has been going on for about a year and two months, ever since the pipe burst forced me into the smaller apartment and my routines fell apart.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
No, not two years or more. It feels like a long time, honestly, but if I’m counting it out, it’s been about a year and two months.

It really started after that pipe burst and I had to move into the smaller apartment. That whole disruption kind of knocked everything out of place, and my routines fell apart after that. Since then it’s just been ongoing. So… over a year, yes, but not two years.

I know that probably sounds like just a practical inconvenience, but after that my routines kind of fell apart, and I never really got them back. Since then it’s just been this steady feeling that I’m behind, that I’m messing things up, that things are my fault. So, yeah… roughly fourteen months.\n\nDoctor: Do you still find pleasure in activities that you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean… I still try sometimes, because I remember those things used to help. Like I’ll put on a record I used to love, or I’ll start cooking something that normally would’ve felt kind of grounding, or I’ll pull out some little electronics thing I was going to tinker with. The things that used to reset me—putting on a record, cooking something, messing around with electronics—don’t really reach me anymore, and it’s similar with people. I can be in the room with friends or family and know, intellectually, that this should feel good or comforting, but inside it’s just kind of dull and heavy. I’m there, but I don’t feel connected to it the way I used to.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're able to enjoy the company of others at all anymore?\n\nPatient: A little, maybe… but not in the way I used to. It’s hard to explain without sounding like I’m being dramatic, but most days I wake up already feeling this flat, heavy sadness, before I’ve even talked to anyone or done anything. So by the time I’m around people, it’s like I’m already behind this thick pane of glass.\n\nI can sit there and know, like, “These are people I care about, this should feel nice, I should be glad they’re here,” but the feeling doesn’t really come through. It’s kind of the same as with the other things I used to rely on to reset myself—putting on a record, cooking something, tinkering with some little electronics project. I still recognize them as good things, but they don’t reach me the way they used to.\n\nSo… I wouldn’t say I enjoy company much anymore. I can tolerate it sometimes, and I don’t want to hurt anyone’s feelings, but it doesn’t lift that heaviness. It just stays there.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any periods in the last year where your symptoms were significantly worse for a few weeks?\n\nPatient: It’s been pretty steadily bad for a long time, honestly. I don’t know if I’d separate it into a neat “few weeks” where it was worse, because it kind of… settled in and stayed.\n\nI’d say this has been going on for about a year and two months now. I can recognize that these are things I used to enjoy, and sometimes I go through the motions because I don’t know what else to do, but the actual pleasure part is mostly missing.\n\nDoctor: Have you had times in the last year where you felt somewhat better for a while, before the sadness came back?\n\nPatient: Not really in any clear way, no. I mean, there are probably individual hours or maybe a day here and there where I can function a little better, or I’m distracted enough that I think, “Maybe I’m coming out of it,” but it doesn’t last long enough to feel like an actual better stretch.\n\nThe bigger thing is that it’s been going on for about a year and two months now, pretty much since the pipe burst and I had to move into that smaller apartment. I keep coming back to that because it’s when everything seemed to come apart. Some days are heavier than others, but the main thing is that it started around then and has just kept going.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any periods in the last year where you felt completely back to normal?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. Not completely back to normal.\n\nThere have been little pockets where I’ve been less aware of it, maybe, or I’ve managed to get through a day without everything feeling quite as stuck. But if you mean really feeling like myself again, like before all of this started, then no. This has been going on for about a year and two months now, since the pipe burst and I had to move into that smaller apartment. I keep mentioning that because it feels like the point where my routines fell apart, and they just never really came back together.\n\nSo even on the better days, I still don’t feel like I’m back to the way I was. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don’t think there was a real point where I was getting better and then it came back, no. It’s been more… steady, I guess, in a bad way. Maybe some days felt a little less intense, or I’d manage better if I had enough distractions, but I wouldn’t call that a recovery or even a clear improvement.

It’s been going on for about a year and two months now, basically since the pipe burst and I had to move into the smaller apartment. That change sounds like just a housing thing when I say it out loud, but it really threw off all my routines, and once those fell apart I never felt like I got my footing back.

So, no, I can’t really point to a period where I thought, “Okay, I’m getting better,” and then it returned. It’s more like it started around then and just kept going.

Since then it’s just been this steady feeling that I’m behind, that I’m messing things up, that things are my fault. So, yeah… roughly fourteen months.\n\nDoctor: Do you still find pleasure in activities that you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean… I still try sometimes, because I remember those things used to help. Like I’ll put on a record I used to love, or I’ll start cooking something that normally would’ve felt kind of grounding, or I’ll pull out some little electronics thing I was going to tinker with. But most days I wake up with this flat, heavy sadness, and those things just don’t seem to reach me anymore.\n\nIt’s strange because I can remember liking them. I know, objectively, that they were things I enjoyed. But when I do them now, it’s like there’s a wall there. I’m going through the motions, waiting for that little feeling of relief or interest to show up, and it just… doesn’t.\n\nDoctor: Do you still enjoy spending time with friends and family?\n\nPatient: Not like I used to, no. I mean, I still care about them, I know I do, and sometimes I’ll answer a text or sit with people if they come by, but it doesn’t really feel… relieving, I guess.\n\nMost days I wake up with this flat, heavy sadness already there, before anything has even happened, and it kind of colors everything. The things that used to reset me—putting on a record, cooking something, messing around with electronics—don’t really reach me anymore, and it’s similar with people. I can be in the room with friends or family and know, intellectually, that this should feel good or comforting, but inside it’s just kind of dull and heavy. I’m there, but I don’t feel connected to it the way I used to.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're able to enjoy the company of others at all anymore?\n\nPatient: A little, maybe… but not in the way I used to. So by the time I’m around people, it’s like I’m already behind this thick pane of glass.\n\nI can sit there and know, like, “These are people I care about, this should feel nice, I should be glad they’re here,” but the feeling doesn’t really come through. It’s kind of the same as with the other things I used to rely on to reset myself—putting on a record, cooking something, tinkering with some little electronics project. I still recognize them as good things, but they don’t reach me the way they used to.\n\nSo… I wouldn’t say I enjoy company much anymore. I can tolerate it sometimes, and I don’t want to hurt anyone’s feelings, but it doesn’t lift that heaviness. It just stays there.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any periods in the last year where your symptoms were significantly worse for a few weeks?\n\nPatient: It’s been pretty steadily bad for a long time, honestly. I don’t know if I’d separate it into a neat “few weeks” where it was worse, because it kind of… settled in and stayed.\n\nI’d say this has been going on for about a year and two months now. It started after that pipe burst forced me out of my place and into the smaller apartment. That sounds like such a practical thing, like just a housing problem, but it threw everything off. My routines fell apart, the little structure I had during the day disappeared, and since then it’s just felt like I haven’t really gotten back to myself.\n\nSo, yes, there have been times where it felt heavier, but the bigger thing is that it’s been there for about fourteen months, pretty continuously.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still find pleasure in?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I keep trying the same things because part of me remembers that they used to help. Like, I’ll put on a record and think maybe if I just sit with it long enough something will click, or I’ll start cooking something because that used to feel grounding, like there was a rhythm to it. Or I’ll pull out some little electronics thing to tinker with, because that used to make me lose track of time in a good way.\n\nBut most days I wake up with this flat, heavy sadness already there, and it’s like everything has to pass through that first. The music is still music, the food still smells like it should, the project still makes sense in my head, but it just doesn’t… reach me. I can recognize that these are things I used to enjoy, and sometimes I go through the motions because I don’t know what else to do, but the actual pleasure part is mostly missing.\n\nDoctor: Have you had times in the last year where you felt somewhat better for a while, before the sadness came back?\n\nPatient: Not really in any clear way, no. I mean, there are probably individual hours or maybe a day here and there where I can function a little better, or I’m distracted enough that I think, “Maybe I’m coming out of it,” but it doesn’t last long enough to feel like an actual better stretch.\n\nThe bigger thing is that it’s been going on for about a year and two months now, pretty much since the pipe burst and I had to move into that smaller apartment. I keep coming back to that because it’s when everything seemed to come apart. My routines were disrupted, the space felt wrong, and I never really got my footing back after that.\n\nSo I wouldn’t say there was a period where I got better and then the sadness came back. It’s more like it settled in around then and has just stayed, even if some days are a little less heavy than others.\n\nDoctor: Have your symptoms gotten worse over the past fourteen months?\n\nPatient: I think… yes and no. It’s hard to measure because it didn’t feel like one straight drop every month, like I could point to March versus July and say, “There, that was worse.” It’s more that this has been going on for about a year and two months now, ever since the pipe burst and I had to leave my place and move into that smaller apartment.\n\nAt first I kept thinking it was just the disruption, you know, like once I unpacked or got used to the new space, I’d settle back into my routines. But the routines kind of fell apart and never really came back the same way. So over those fourteen months, it feels like it became more fixed, more settled in. Some days are heavier than others, but the main thing is that it started around then and has just kept going.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any periods in the last year where you felt completely back to normal?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. Not completely back to normal.\n\nThere have been little pockets where I’ve been less aware of it, maybe, or I’ve managed to get through a day without everything feeling quite as stuck. But if you mean really feeling like myself again, like before all of this started, then no. This has been going on for about a year and two months now, since the pipe burst and I had to move into that smaller apartment. I keep mentioning that because it feels like the point where my routines fell apart, and they just never really came back together.\n\nSo even on the better days, I still don’t feel like I’m back to the way I was. It’s more like I’m functioning around it, not actually normal again.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any distinct periods in the last year where you felt depressed, followed by periods where you felt okay again?\n\nPatient: No, not really distinct periods like that. I’ve had days where I’m a little more functional, or maybe I get through work or errands and it feels less intense for a while, but I wouldn’t say there was a real stretch where I felt okay again and then later got depressed again.\n\nIt’s more like… this started about a year and two months ago, when the pipe burst and I had to move into that smaller apartment, and after that my routines just fell apart. Since then it’s sort of been one long period, with some days better or worse, but not like separate episodes with normal time in between.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any days in the last year where you didn't feel sad or heavy at all?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. Like, I’ll put on a record and think maybe if I just sit with it long enough something will click, or I’ll start cooking something because that used to feel grounding, like there was a rhythm to it. Or I’ll pull out some little electronics thing to tinker with, because that used to make me lose track of time in a good way.\n\nBut most days I wake up with this flat, heavy sadness already there, and it’s like everything has to pass through that first. The music is still music, the food still smells like it should, the project still makes sense in my head, but it just doesn’t… reach me. I can recognize that these are things I used to enjoy, and sometimes I go through the motions because I don’t know what else to do, but the actual pleasure part is mostly missing.\n\nDoctor: Have you had times in the last year where you felt somewhat better for a while, before the sadness came back?\n\nPatient: Not really in any clear way, no. I mean, there are probably individual hours or maybe a day here and there where I can function a little better, or I’m distracted enough that I think, “Maybe I’m coming out of it,” but it doesn’t last long enough to feel like an actual better stretch.\n\nThe bigger thing is that it’s been going on for about a year and two months now, pretty much since the pipe burst and I had to move into that smaller apartment. I keep coming back to that because it’s when everything seemed to come apart. My routines were disrupted, the space felt wrong, and I never really got my footing back after that.\n\nSo I wouldn’t say there was a period where I got better and then the sadness came back. It’s more like it settled in around then and has just stayed, even if some days are a little less heavy than others.\n\nDoctor: Have your symptoms gotten worse over the past fourteen months?\n\nPatient: I think… yes and no. It’s hard to measure because it didn’t feel like one straight drop every month, like I could point to March versus July and say, “There, that was worse.” It’s more that this has been going on for about a year and two months now, ever since the pipe burst and I had to leave my place and move into that smaller apartment.\n\nAt first I kept thinking it was just the disruption, you know, like once I unpacked or got used to the new space, I’d settle back into my routines. But the routines kind of fell apart and never really came back the same way. So over those fourteen months, it feels like it became more fixed, more settled in. Some days are heavier than others, but the main thing is that it started around then and has just kept going.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any periods in the last year where you felt completely back to normal?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. Not completely back to normal.\n\nThere have been little pockets where I’ve been less aware of it, maybe, or I’ve managed to get through a day without everything feeling quite as stuck. But if you mean really feeling like myself again, like before all of this started, then no. This has been going on for about a year and two months now, since the pipe burst and I had to move into that smaller apartment. I keep mentioning that because it feels like the point where my routines fell apart, and they just never really came back together.\n\nSo even on the better days, I still don’t feel like I’m back to the way I was. It’s more like I’m functioning around it, not actually normal again.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any distinct periods in the last year where you felt depressed, followed by periods where you felt okay again?\n\nPatient: No, not really distinct periods like that. I’ve had days where I’m a little more functional, or maybe I get through work or errands and it feels less intense for a while, but I wouldn’t say there was a real stretch where I felt okay again and then later got depressed again.\n\nIt’s more like… this started about a year and two months ago, when the pipe burst and I had to move into that smaller apartment, and after that my routines just fell apart. Since then it’s sort of been one long period, with some days better or worse, but not like separate episodes with normal time in between.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any days in the last year where you didn't feel sad or heavy at all?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes, two years or less. It’s been about a year and two months, as best as I can count it.

I keep tying it back to when that pipe burst and I had to move into the smaller apartment, because that was really when everything changed. My routines kind of fell apart after that, and I never really got them back in place. So it feels like it’s been going on forever sometimes, but no, it hasn’t been two years. It’s more like a little over a year.

[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n17\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF32.1, F33.1\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I guess… the main reason I came in is that I just can’t get out from under this feeling that I’ve really messed everything up. Like I’ve fallen behind at work, and I know people depend on me there, and then things at my apartment have gotten out of control too, and it all feels like it’s my fault.\n\nIt’s not just feeling stressed or embarrassed. It’s more like this heavy certainty that I’ve ruined things for everyone around me, and that whatever happens next—if something bad happens—I probably deserve it. I know that may sound dramatic, but that’s honestly where my head keeps going.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way?\n\nPatient: It’s been… a little over a year, I guess. About a year and two months now.\n\nIt really started after the pipe burst at my old place and I had to move into the smaller apartment. I know that probably sounds like just a practical inconvenience, but after that my routines kind of fell apart, and I never really got them back. Since then it’s just been this steady feeling that I’m behind, that I’m messing things up, that things are my fault. So, yeah… roughly fourteen months.\n\nDoctor: Do you still find pleasure in activities that you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean… I still try sometimes, because I remember those things used to help. Like I’ll put on a record I used to love, or I’ll start cooking something that normally would’ve felt kind of grounding, or I’ll pull out some little electronics thing I was going to tinker with. But most days I wake up with this flat, heavy sadness, and those things just don’t seem to reach me anymore.\n\nIt’s strange because I can remember liking them. I know, objectively, that they were things I enjoyed. But when I do them now, it’s like there’s a wall there. I’m going through the motions, waiting for that little feeling of relief or interest to show up, and it just… doesn’t.\n\nDoctor: Do you still enjoy spending time with friends and family?\n\nPatient: Not like I used to, no. I mean, I still care about them, I know I do, and sometimes I’ll answer a text or sit with people if they come by, but it doesn’t really feel… relieving, I guess.\n\nMost days I wake up with this flat, heavy sadness already there, before anything has even happened, and it kind of colors everything. The things that used to reset me—putting on a record, cooking something, messing around with electronics—don’t really reach me anymore, and it’s similar with people. I can be in the room with friends or family and know, intellectually, that this should feel good or comforting, but inside it’s just kind of dull and heavy. I’m there, but I don’t feel connected to it the way I used to.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're able to enjoy the company of others at all anymore?\n\nPatient: A little, maybe… but not in the way I used to. It’s hard to explain without sounding like I’m being dramatic, but most days I wake up already feeling this flat, heavy sadness, before I’ve even talked to anyone or done anything. So by the time I’m around people, it’s like I’m already behind this thick pane of glass.\n\nI can sit there and know, like, “These are people I care about, this should feel nice, I should be glad they’re here,” but the feeling doesn’t really come through. It’s kind of the same as with the other things I used to rely on to reset myself—putting on a record, cooking something, tinkering with some little electronics project. I still recognize them as good things, but they don’t reach me the way they used to.\n\nSo… I wouldn’t say I enjoy company much anymore. I can tolerate it sometimes, and I don’t want to hurt anyone’s feelings, but it doesn’t lift that heaviness. It just stays there.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any periods in the last year where your symptoms were significantly worse for a few weeks?\n\nPatient: It’s been pretty steadily bad for a long time, honestly. I don’t know if I’d separate it into a neat “few weeks” where it was worse, because it kind of… settled in and stayed.\n\nI’d say this has been going on for about a year and two months now. It started after that pipe burst forced me out of my place and into the smaller apartment. That sounds like such a practical thing, like just a housing problem, but it threw everything off. My routines fell apart, the little structure I had during the day disappeared, and since then it’s just felt like I haven’t really gotten back to myself.\n\nSo, yes, there have been times where it felt heavier, but the bigger thing is that it’s been there for about fourteen months, pretty continuously.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still find pleasure in?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I keep trying the same things because part of me remembers that they used to help. Like, I’ll put on a record and think maybe if I just sit with it long enough something will click, or I’ll start cooking something because that used to feel grounding, like there was a rhythm to it. Or I’ll pull out some little electronics thing to tinker with, because that used to make me lose track of time in a good way.\n\nBut most days I wake up with this flat, heavy sadness already there, and it’s like everything has to pass through that first. The music is still music, the food still smells like it should, the project still makes sense in my head, but it just doesn’t… reach me. I can recognize that these are things I used to enjoy, and sometimes I go through the motions because I don’t know what else to do, but the actual pleasure part is mostly missing.\n\nDoctor: Have you had times in the last year where you felt somewhat better for a while, before the sadness came back?\n\nPatient: Not really in any clear way, no. I mean, there are probably individual hours or maybe a day here and there where I can function a little better, or I’m distracted enough that I think, “Maybe I’m coming out of it,” but it doesn’t last long enough to feel like an actual better stretch.\n\nThe bigger thing is that it’s been going on for about a year and two months now, pretty much since the pipe burst and I had to move into that smaller apartment. I keep coming back to that because it’s when everything seemed to come apart. My routines were disrupted, the space felt wrong, and I never really got my footing back after that.\n\nSo I wouldn’t say there was a period where I got better and then the sadness came back. It’s more like it settled in around then and has just stayed, even if some days are a little less heavy than others.\n\nDoctor: Have your symptoms gotten worse over the past fourteen months?\n\nPatient: I think… yes and no. It’s hard to measure because it didn’t feel like one straight drop every month, like I could point to March versus July and say, “There, that was worse.” It’s more that this has been going on for about a year and two months now, ever since the pipe burst and I had to leave my place and move into that smaller apartment.\n\nAt first I kept thinking it was just the disruption, you know, like once I unpacked or got used to the new space, I’d settle back into my routines. But the routines kind of fell apart and never really came back the same way. So over those fourteen months, it feels like it became more fixed, more settled in. Some days are heavier than others, but the main thing is that it started around then and has just kept going.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any periods in the last year where you felt completely back to normal?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. Not completely back to normal.\n\nThere have been little pockets where I’ve been less aware of it, maybe, or I’ve managed to get through a day without everything feeling quite as stuck. But if you mean really feeling like myself again, like before all of this started, then no. This has been going on for about a year and two months now, since the pipe burst and I had to move into that smaller apartment. I keep mentioning that because it feels like the point where my routines fell apart, and they just never really came back together.\n\nSo even on the better days, I still don’t feel like I’m back to the way I was. It’s more like I’m functioning around it, not actually normal again.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any distinct periods in the last year where you felt depressed, followed by periods where you felt okay again?\n\nPatient: No, not really distinct periods like that. I’ve had days where I’m a little more functional, or maybe I get through work or errands and it feels less intense for a while, but I wouldn’t say there was a real stretch where I felt okay again and then later got depressed again.\n\nIt’s more like… this started about a year and two months ago, when the pipe burst and I had to move into that smaller apartment, and after that my routines just fell apart. Since then it’s sort of been one long period, with some days better or worse, but not like separate episodes with normal time in between.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any days in the last year where you didn't feel sad or heavy at all?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, there are days where I’m distracted enough that I’m not actively thinking, “I’m sad,” every minute, but if I really check in with myself, most days I wake up with this flat, heavy kind of sadness already there.\n\nIt’s not always dramatic or crying or anything like that. It’s more like… everything has a weight to it before the day even starts. The things that used to sort of reset me don’t seem to work the same way anymore. I used to put on a record and actually feel it change the room a little, or I’d cook something, or mess around with some electronics project, and it would pull me back into myself. Lately those things just don’t reach me. I can do them, sometimes, but it’s like they don’t get through.\n\nSo I don’t think I’ve had a day where there was none of that heaviness at all. Maybe lighter days, but not completely gone.\n\nDoctor: Are your feelings of sadness usually present for most of the day, or do they come and go?\n\nPatient: Most of the day, I think. It can sort of fade into the background if I’m busy or distracted, but it doesn’t really come and go in a clean way.\n\nMost mornings I wake up and it’s already there, this flat, heavy sadness. Not always sharp or overwhelming, but like a weight over everything. And even when I try to do the things that used to help—putting on a record, cooking something, tinkering with some electronics—it’s like they don’t reach the same part of me anymore. I might go through the motions, but the feeling is still sitting there underneath.\n\nSo I wouldn’t say it just comes and goes. It’s usually present, maybe quieter at times, but still there.\n\nDoctor: Have your symptoms lasted for two years or more?\n\nPatient: No, not two years or more. It feels like a long time, honestly, but if I’m counting it out, it’s been about a year and two months.\n\nIt really started after that pipe burst and I had to move into the smaller apartment. That whole disruption kind of knocked everything out of place, and my routines fell apart after that. Since then it’s just been ongoing. So… over a year, yes, but not two years.\n\nDoctor: Has there been any point in the last year where you felt like you were getting better, and then your symptoms returned?\n\nPatient: I don’t think there was a real point where I was getting better and then it came back, no. It’s been more… steady, I guess, in a bad way. Maybe some days felt a little less intense, or I’d manage better if I had enough distractions, but I wouldn’t call that a recovery or even a clear improvement.\n\nIt’s been going on for about a year and two months now, basically since the pipe burst and I had to move into the smaller apartment. That change sounds like just a housing thing when I say it out loud, but it really threw off all my routines, and once those fell apart I never felt like I got my footing back.\n\nSo, no, I can’t really point to a period where I thought, “Okay, I’m getting better,” and then it returned. It’s more like it started around then and just kept going.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any significant improvement in your symptoms at any point over the last year?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean, there have been little stretches where I functioned a bit better, maybe because I had to deal with work or errands or something practical, but I wouldn’t call that significant improvement.\n\nIt’s been going on for about a year and two months now, since that pipe burst forced me out and I ended up in the smaller apartment. That was when my routines really fell apart, and I don’t feel like I ever got them back in a steady way. So if you’re asking whether there was a point in the last year where things clearly lifted or I felt more like myself again… no, I can’t honestly say that happened.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt this way for two years or less?\n\nPatient: Yes, two years or less. It’s been about a year and two months, as best as I can count it.\n\nI keep tying it back to when that pipe burst and I had to move into the smaller apartment, because that was really when everything changed. My routines kind of fell apart after that, and I never really got them back in place. So it feels like it’s been going on forever sometimes, but no, it hasn’t been two years. It’s more like a little over a year.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

I mean, I still care about them, I know I do, and sometimes I’ll answer a text or sit with people if they come by, but it doesn’t really feel… relieving, I guess.\n\nMost days I wake up with this flat, heavy sadness already there, before anything has even happened, and it kind of colors everything. The things that used to reset me—putting on a record, cooking something, messing around with electronics—don’t really reach me anymore, and it’s similar with people. I can be in the room with friends or family and know, intellectually, that this should feel good or comforting, but inside it’s just kind of dull and heavy. I’m there, but I don’t feel connected to it the way I used to.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're able to enjoy the company of others at all anymore?\n\nPatient: A little, maybe… but not in the way I used to. It’s hard to explain without sounding like I’m being dramatic, but most days I wake up already feeling this flat, heavy sadness, before I’ve even talked to anyone or done anything. So by the time I’m around people, it’s like I’m already behind this thick pane of glass.\n\nI can sit there and know, like, “These are people I care about, this should feel nice, I should be glad they’re here,” but the feeling doesn’t really come through. It’s kind of the same as with the other things I used to rely on to reset myself—putting on a record, cooking something, tinkering with some little electronics project. I still recognize them as good things, but they don’t reach me the way they used to.\n\nSo… I wouldn’t say I enjoy company much anymore. I can tolerate it sometimes, and I don’t want to hurt anyone’s feelings, but it doesn’t lift that heaviness. It just stays there.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any periods in the last year where your symptoms were significantly worse for a few weeks?\n\nPatient: It’s been pretty steadily bad for a long time, honestly. I don’t know if I’d separate it into a neat “few weeks” where it was worse, because it kind of… settled in and stayed.\n\nI’d say this has been going on for about a year and two months now. It started after that pipe burst forced me out of my place and into the smaller apartment. That sounds like such a practical thing, like just a housing problem, but it threw everything off. My routines fell apart, the little structure I had during the day disappeared, and since then it’s just felt like I haven’t really gotten back to myself.\n\nSo, yes, there have been times where it felt heavier, but the bigger thing is that it’s been there for about fourteen months, pretty continuously.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still find pleasure in?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I keep trying the same things because part of me remembers that they used to help. Like, I’ll put on a record and think maybe if I just sit with it long enough something will click, or I’ll start cooking something because that used to feel grounding, like there was a rhythm to it. Or I’ll pull out some little electronics thing to tinker with, because that used to make me lose track of time in a good way.\n\nBut most days I wake up with this flat, heavy sadness already there, and it’s like everything has to pass through that first. The music is still music, the food still smells like it should, the project still makes sense in my head, but it just doesn’t… reach me. I can recognize that these are things I used to enjoy, and sometimes I go through the motions because I don’t know what else to do, but the actual pleasure part is mostly missing.\n\nDoctor: Have you had times in the last year where you felt somewhat better for a while, before the sadness came back?\n\nPatient: Not really in any clear way, no. I mean, there are probably individual hours or maybe a day here and there where I can function a little better, or I’m distracted enough that I think, “Maybe I’m coming out of it,” but it doesn’t last long enough to feel like an actual better stretch.\n\nThe bigger thing is that it’s been going on for about a year and two months now, pretty much since the pipe burst and I had to move into that smaller apartment. I keep coming back to that because it’s when everything seemed to come apart. My routines were disrupted, the space felt wrong, and I never really got my footing back after that.\n\nSo I wouldn’t say there was a period where I got better and then the sadness came back. It’s more like it settled in around then and has just stayed, even if some days are a little less heavy than others.\n\nDoctor: Have your symptoms gotten worse over the past fourteen months?\n\nPatient: I think… yes and no. It’s hard to measure because it didn’t feel like one straight drop every month, like I could point to March versus July and say, “There, that was worse.” It’s more that this has been going on for about a year and two months now, ever since the pipe burst and I had to leave my place and move into that smaller apartment.\n\nAt first I kept thinking it was just the disruption, you know, like once I unpacked or got used to the new space, I’d settle back into my routines. But the routines kind of fell apart and never really came back the same way. So over those fourteen months, it feels like it became more fixed, more settled in. Some days are heavier than others, but the main thing is that it started around then and has just kept going.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any periods in the last year where you felt completely back to normal?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. Not completely back to normal.\n\nThere have been little pockets where I’ve been less aware of it, maybe, or I’ve managed to get through a day without everything feeling quite as stuck. But if you mean really feeling like myself again, like before all of this started, then no. This has been going on for about a year and two months now, since the pipe burst and I had to move into that smaller apartment. I keep mentioning that because it feels like the point where my routines fell apart, and they just never really came back together.\n\nSo even on the better days, I still don’t feel like I’m back to the way I was. It’s more like I’m functioning around it, not actually normal again.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any distinct periods in the last year where you felt depressed, followed by periods where you felt okay again?\n\nPatient: No, not really distinct periods like that. I’ve had days where I’m a little more functional, or maybe I get through work or errands and it feels less intense for a while, but I wouldn’t say there was a real stretch where I felt okay again and then later got depressed again.\n\nIt’s more like… this started about a year and two months ago, when the pipe burst and I had to move into that smaller apartment, and after that my routines just fell apart. Since then it’s sort of been one long period, with some days better or worse, but not like separate episodes with normal time in between.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any days in the last year where you didn't feel sad or heavy at all?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, there are days where I’m distracted enough that I’m not actively thinking, “I’m sad,” every minute, but if I really check in with myself, most days I wake up with this flat, heavy kind of sadness already there.\n\nIt’s not always dramatic or crying or anything like that. It’s more like… everything has a weight to it before the day even starts. The things that used to sort of reset me don’t seem to work the same way anymore. I used to put on a record and actually feel it change the room a little, or I’d cook something, or mess around with some electronics project, and it would pull me back into myself. Lately those things just don’t reach me. I can do them, sometimes, but it’s like they don’t get through.\n\nSo I don’t think I’ve had a day where there was none of that heaviness at all. Maybe lighter days, but not completely gone.\n\nDoctor: Are your feelings of sadness usually present for most of the day, or do they come and go?\n\nPatient: Most of the day, I think. It can sort of fade into the background if I’m busy or distracted, but it doesn’t really come and go in a clean way.\n\nMost mornings I wake up and it’s already there, this flat, heavy sadness. Not always sharp or overwhelming, but like a weight over everything. And even when I try to do the things that used to help—putting on a record, cooking something, tinkering with some electronics—it’s like they don’t reach the same part of me anymore. I might go through the motions, but the feeling is still sitting there underneath.\n\nSo I wouldn’t say it just comes and goes. It’s usually present, maybe quieter at times, but still there.\n\nDoctor: Have your symptoms lasted for two years or more?\n\nPatient: No, not two years or more. It feels like a long time, honestly, but if I’m counting it out, it’s been about a year and two months.\n\nIt really started after that pipe burst and I had to move into the smaller apartment. That whole disruption kind of knocked everything out of place, and my routines fell apart after that. Since then it’s just been ongoing. So… over a year, yes, but not two years.\n\nDoctor: Has there been any point in the last year where you felt like you were getting better, and then your symptoms returned?\n\nPatient: I don’t think there was a real point where I was getting better and then it came back, no. It’s been more… steady, I guess, in a bad way. Maybe some days felt a little less intense, or I’d manage better if I had enough distractions, but I wouldn’t call that a recovery or even a clear improvement.\n\nIt’s been going on for about a year and two months now, basically since the pipe burst and I had to move into the smaller apartment. That change sounds like just a housing thing when I say it out loud, but it really threw off all my routines, and once those fell apart I never felt like I got my footing back.\n\nSo, no, I can’t really point to a period where I thought, “Okay, I’m getting better,” and then it returned. It’s more like it started around then and just kept going.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any significant improvement in your symptoms at any point over the last year?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean, there have been little stretches where I functioned a bit better, maybe because I had to deal with work or errands or something practical, but I wouldn’t call that significant improvement.\n\nIt’s been going on for about a year and two months now, since that pipe burst forced me out and I ended up in the smaller apartment. That was when my routines really fell apart, and I don’t feel like I ever got them back in a steady way. So if you’re asking whether there was a point in the last year where things clearly lifted or I felt more like myself again… no, I can’t honestly say that happened.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt this way for two years or less?\n\nPatient: Yes, two years or less. It’s been about a year and two months, as best as I can count it.\n\nI keep tying it back to when that pipe burst and I had to move into the smaller apartment, because that was really when everything changed. My routines kind of fell apart after that, and I never really got them back in place. So it feels like it’s been going on forever sometimes, but no, it hasn’t been two years. It’s more like a little over a year.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period of at least a couple of months where your symptoms seemed to significantly improve?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. I mean, not for a couple of months where I could say things significantly improved.\n\nLike I said, this has been going on for about a year and two months now, since the pipe burst and I had to move into the smaller apartment. That was kind of the dividing line for me, because my routines fell apart after that, and I never really got them back in a way that felt stable. There might have been individual days, or maybe a week here and there, where things felt a little less heavy or I was more distracted, but not a couple of months of real improvement. Not anything I’d look back on and say, “That was a better period.”\n\nDoctor: Have you felt this persistent sadness or heaviness for the majority of every day for the past two weeks?\n\nPatient: Yes… most days, yes. It’s not always dramatic, like I’m crying all day or anything like that, but it’s this flat, heavy sadness that’s just kind of there when I wake up.\n\nIt’s like I open my eyes and I can already feel it sitting on me. And things that used to help me reset a little—putting on a record, cooking something, messing around with electronics or trying to fix some little gadget—those things don’t really reach me the same way anymore. I’ll do them sometimes, or think about doing them, but the heaviness is still there underneath.\n\nSo for the past two weeks, yeah, I’d say it’s been there most of the day, most days.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. Like I’ve fallen behind at work, and I know people depend on me there, and then things at my apartment have gotten out of control too, and it all feels like it’s my fault.\n\nIt’s not just feeling stressed or embarrassed. It’s more like this heavy certainty that I’ve ruined things for everyone around me, and that whatever happens next—if something bad happens—I probably deserve it. I know that may sound dramatic, but that’s honestly where my head keeps going.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way?\n\nPatient: It’s been… a little over a year, I guess. About a year and two months now.\n\nIt really started after the pipe burst at my old place and I had to move into the smaller apartment. I know that probably sounds like just a practical inconvenience, but after that my routines kind of fell apart, and I never really got them back. Since then it’s just been this steady feeling that I’m behind, that I’m messing things up, that things are my fault. So, yeah… roughly fourteen months.\n\nDoctor: Do you still find pleasure in activities that you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean… I still try sometimes, because I remember those things used to help. Like I’ll put on a record I used to love, or I’ll start cooking something that normally would’ve felt kind of grounding, or I’ll pull out some little electronics thing I was going to tinker with. But most days I wake up with this flat, heavy sadness, and those things just don’t seem to reach me anymore.\n\nIt’s strange because I can remember liking them. I know, objectively, that they were things I enjoyed. But when I do them now, it’s like there’s a wall there. I’m going through the motions, waiting for that little feeling of relief or interest to show up, and it just… doesn’t.\n\nDoctor: Do you still enjoy spending time with friends and family?\n\nPatient: Not like I used to, no. The things that used to reset me—putting on a record, cooking something, messing around with electronics—don’t really reach me anymore, and it’s similar with people. I can be in the room with friends or family and know, intellectually, that this should feel good or comforting, but inside it’s just kind of dull and heavy. I still recognize them as good things, but they don’t reach me the way they used to.\n\nSo… I wouldn’t say I enjoy company much anymore. I can tolerate it sometimes, and I don’t want to hurt anyone’s feelings, but it doesn’t lift that heaviness. It just stays there.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any periods in the last year where your symptoms were significantly worse for a few weeks?\n\nPatient: It’s been pretty steadily bad for a long time, honestly. I don’t know if I’d separate it into a neat “few weeks” where it was worse, because it kind of… settled in and stayed.\n\nI’d say this has been going on for about a year and two months now. It started after that pipe burst forced me out of my place and into the smaller apartment. That sounds like such a practical thing, like just a housing problem, but it threw everything off. My routines fell apart, the little structure I had during the day disappeared, and since then it’s just felt like I haven’t really gotten back to myself.\n\nSo, yes, there have been times where it felt heavier, but the bigger thing is that it’s been there for about fourteen months, pretty continuously.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still find pleasure in?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I keep trying the same things because part of me remembers that they used to help. Like, I’ll put on a record and think maybe if I just sit with it long enough something will click, or I’ll start cooking something because that used to feel grounding, like there was a rhythm to it. Or I’ll pull out some little electronics thing to tinker with, because that used to make me lose track of time in a good way.\n\nBut most days I wake up with this flat, heavy sadness already there, and it’s like everything has to pass through that first. The music is still music, the food still smells like it should, the project still makes sense in my head, but it just doesn’t… reach me. I can recognize that these are things I used to enjoy, and sometimes I go through the motions because I don’t know what else to do, but the actual pleasure part is mostly missing.\n\nDoctor: Have you had times in the last year where you felt somewhat better for a while, before the sadness came back?\n\nPatient: Not really in any clear way, no. I mean, there are probably individual hours or maybe a day here and there where I can function a little better, or I’m distracted enough that I think, “Maybe I’m coming out of it,” but it doesn’t last long enough to feel like an actual better stretch.\n\nThe bigger thing is that it’s been going on for about a year and two months now, pretty much since the pipe burst and I had to move into that smaller apartment. I keep coming back to that because it’s when everything seemed to come apart. My routines were disrupted, the space felt wrong, and I never really got my footing back after that.\n\nSo I wouldn’t say there was a period where I got better and then the sadness came back. It’s more like it settled in around then and has just stayed, even if some days are a little less heavy than others.\n\nDoctor: Have your symptoms gotten worse over the past fourteen months?\n\nPatient: I think… yes and no. Not completely back to normal.\n\nThere have been little pockets where I’ve been less aware of it, maybe, or I’ve managed to get through a day without everything feeling quite as stuck.
